- Check that with H and F removed, there remains any path I→G? G still connects to X, but I has no route to X (needs F or H). Therefore all paths are broken.

Verify minimality:
- Single-node removals fail:
  - Remove H: I–P–F–G reaches G.
  - Remove F: I–H–G is intact.
  - Remove X: I–H–G still works.
  - Remove P or J: I–H–G still works.
  - Remove B/C/E/O/N etc.: I–H–G still works.
Therefore at least two nodes are required.

- Any 2-node cut must include H or F because I–H–G and I–P–F–G are two internally node-disjoint paths. Removing only one of them leaves the other. The pair [H, F] indeed disconnects, and no single node suffices.

2) Through I: B–I–J–M or B–I–P–J–M exist. If E is removed, paths via I could still reach J then M. Cutting I blocks all I-based routes.
3) Through C or O: From C or O you can go to I (via C–I or O–P–J–M paths). With I removed, these cannot reach J/M; with E removed, they also cannot go via E. Thus C and O routes are blocked by removing E and I.

Verify minimality:
- If we remove only E, B–I–J–M remains, so not enough.
- If we remove only I, B–E–M remains, so not enough.
- Removing both E and I disconnects all routes from B to M since the only neighbors of B left (C and O) cannot reach M without passing through I (to J) or E.

Therefore the minimum cut set size is 2.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, I]

- Explore all paths from N up toward that region:

From N, neighbors: C, O, Y, B. Node Y only connects to N and O, so any route from N to the rest goes through C, O, or B.

Check whether there are bottleneck edges closer to N:
- Edge (N, B): If we remove it, N can still go N–C–B or N–O–P–J–I–C–B etc., so not sufficient alone.
- Edge (N, C): N can still go N–B–… or N–O–…; not sufficient.
- Edge (N, O): N can still go via B or C; not sufficient.

Observe that to reach G’s neighborhood, every route from N must pass through B or H on the way to X/K/F/H because:
- From the lower part, upward connections are via nodes B, C, I, J, P, O to the upper left.
- The only connections entering the “G side” are edges touching H or F (since X and K connect only to F and G). The only edge from the central graph to H is (H, B). From the central graph to F is (F, E) and (F, G); but from N’s side to reach F without passing H, paths go through E. E connects down only to B and I; from N to E you must pass via B or I, and I connects to B and J and P; but to reach E from I there is edge (E, I). Still, any route from N to E or H must touch B or I; however, to reach G’s neighborhood, the only gateways are:
  - via H using edge (H, G) or (H, B) onward,
  - via F using edge (F, E). From N to F you must use edge (E, F), hence you must reach E first via B or I.

Thus, all N→G paths must pass through at least one of the two edges: (H, B) or (E, B). Why (E, B)? Because E connects to F, L, M, I, and B; from N’s side, to get to E you must come through B or I; but from I to E there is direct edge (I, E) that avoids B. We must ensure coverage of I route:
- N→O→P→I→E→F→G is a valid path that bypasses (H, B) and (E, B). So we need to block access either I–E or E–F or somewhere else.

Find a minimal cut:
Consider cutting three strategic edges that block all gateways to G’s neighbors:
- Cut (H, G) to stop paths via H directly into G.
- Cut (F, G) to stop direct F→G.
- But G also connects to X and K; however those are only reachable from the rest through F or H because X and K connect only to F and G (K also to X and G? K connects to F and X; not to others). Therefore, if both connections from the rest of graph to {F, H} are severed, G is isolated.

Instead of removing edges incident to G, we can cut the two edges that connect the rest to {F, H}:
- Connections from the main graph to H: only edge (H, B) and (H, G). From outside, H is reached via B; so removing (H, B) isolates H from the rest except G.
- Connections from the main graph to F: only edge (F, E) and (F, G) and (F, K) and (F, X). The rest of the graph reaches F only through E; K and X are leaves attached to F/G cluster. So removing (E, F) isolates F (except to G, K, X).

If we remove both (H, B) and (E, F), then from N’s side you cannot reach H or F; thus you cannot reach X or K or G through F/H. Since G’s only neighbors remaining to the rest would be just F and H, which are now disconnected from the main graph, all N→G paths are broken.

Verify no remaining path:
- Any N→…→B→…→H route is blocked at (H, B).
- Any N→…→I→E→F route is blocked at (E, F).
- There is no other edge into X or K except via F (and G), so blocked.
- Direct edges to G from the rest do not exist except F and H; both separated.

Minimality:
- Removing only one of (H, B) or (E, F) leaves a path: if only (H, B) is removed, path N–O–P–I–E–F–G exists. If only (E, F) is removed, path N–B–H–G exists. Therefore at least 2 edges are required, and the pair [(H, B), (E, F)] suffices.

Final Answer:

- If we remove J and P, remaining gateway is C. Is G connected to C? Yes: G–H–C or G–K–J (but J removed) however G–H–C exists. So [J, P] fails.
- If we remove C and P, remaining gateway is J. Is G connected to J? Yes: G–K–J and G–H–J. So [C, P] fails.

Verify cut [J, C, P]:
- Without J, C, P, node I has no remaining neighbors, so no path from G (or any node) to I exists.
- We are not removing G or I.
- No smaller set works as reasoned above.

Hence earlier assumption incorrect.

So we must block the direct edge (E, H) plus all alternative entries to H or departures from E.

  5) Through F/G/H: E–F–G–H–I–P–J (still uses I–P).
  6) Through D: paths to J–D triangle still require reaching J via D–J edge; but from E’s side to D would have to go … P–D–J (so still meets P).

- Key observation: Every path from the lower-left region (B, C, N, O, H, I, P, F, G, …) to J must enter J via either M or P or D. However, D connects to P and J (triangle D–P–J). Thus any route not using M must pass through P to reach J (possibly via D). Therefore, to disconnect E from J, it suffices to remove the two “gate” nodes M and P.
- Verify completeness:
  • Removing M cuts all paths that use E–M–J or E–L–M–J.
  • Removing P cuts all other routes: any E→… path into the right side reaches J via P (either P–J directly or P–D–J). Without P, there is no connection from I/D side to J, and there is no alternative edge to J except M.
  • After removing {M, P}, there is no remaining E–J path.
- Minimality:
  • A single-node removal cannot work: 
    – Remove M only: paths exist via E–B–C–I–P–J (or via D). 
    – Remove P only: path E–M–J remains. 
  Hence at least 2 nodes are necessary, and {M, P} of size 2 achieves disconnection.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [M, P]

- Exit 2: edge (G, H) is the only link from the small rectangle’s top chain (K–X–G) to the right side; K and X have no other external edges; G’s only external edge is to H. Thus any path via K/X/G must traverse (G, H).

Therefore, cutting both edges (F, E) and (G, H) severs:
- All paths using the bottom route via E (removed by cutting (F, E)).
- All paths using the top route via K/X/G (removed by cutting (G, H)).

Verify no remaining path:
- From F to J via K or X would require reaching H through G; blocked at (G, H).
- From F via E is blocked at (F, E).
- There are no other edges from F (no direct to H/I/P/J; K/X/G only inside the rectangle).
Hence F is disconnected from the rest of the graph that contains J.

Minimality:
- Removing only one of these edges leaves a path: if only (F, E) is cut, path F–X–G–H–I–P–J exists. If only (G, H) is cut, path F–E–B–C–P–J exists. Thus at least 2 edges are necessary, and the pair {(F, E), (G, H)} suffices.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, E), (G, H)]

1) L–E–M–J (direct via M)
2) L–M–J (same as 1)
3) L–E–I–J
4) L–E–I–P–J
5) L–E–B–H–I–J (since H–I edge exists)
6) L–E–B–C–I–J
7) L–E–B–C–O–P–J
8) L–E–B–N–O–P–J
9) L–E–F–G–H–I–J
... (any route from E down the left side eventually meets I or P or M before J)

Observation:
- Every path from L to J must leave L through either E or M (L has degree 2).
- From M there is only edge to E and to J; specifically M connects to E and J. Thus if M remains, path L–M–J exists unless M is removed.
- If M is removed, all remaining paths from L must go through E. Then we must also block all E→J routes.
- Check E→J: E connects to F, B, M, and I. With M removed, E connects to F, B, I.
  - From F, one can go to G→H→I→J.
  - From B, multiple ways reach I or P: B–H–I, B–C–I, B–C–O–P, B–N–O–P, etc., and then to J via I–J or P–J.
  - From I, direct to J or via P.
- So, after removing M, to block E→J we must cut at least one node that all these remaining routes share. The only common node on those routes is I or P? Not common, because via F→G→H→I routes still pass through I before reaching J or P. Paths from B can go to P without I (e.g., B–C–O–P–J). Hence I alone is not enough. Similarly, P alone is not enough because we have E–I–J.
- But all E→J paths must pass through either I or P (since the only direct neighbors of J besides M are I, P, and D via P? J connects to I, P, M, and D; D connects only to P and J, but reaching D from E requires going through P. Therefore, to reach J without M, the last step must be through I or P).
- Therefore, removing both I and P, together with M removed, will disconnect.

Minimality:
- At least one of {E, M} must be removed because L has only E and M; otherwise a path exists through that neighbor. If we remove E instead of M, L–M–J still exists, so we must remove M (or remove J which is forbidden). Thus M is necessary.
- After removing M, we must block both portals to J: I and P, because there exist E→…→I→J and E→…→P→J paths that avoid the other. Therefore, both I and P are necessary.
- No 2-node cut works: if we try {M, I} there remains E→…→P→J; if {M, P} there remains E→…→I→J; if {E, ?} path L–M–J survives unless ? = M.

Hence the minimum cut set size is 3: {M, I, P}.

Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, I, P]

  Combined Level 2 frontier: {H, E, O, I, B}
- Level 3: check if G is reachable.
  - From H: H is directly connected to G. So path N → B → H → G is valid.

Verify edges in the path:
- Is N directly connected to B? Yes (horizontal line).
- Is B directly connected to H? Yes (horizontal line).
- Is H directly connected to G? Yes (vertical line).

No shorter path exists:
- From N, immediate neighbors are Y, B, C; none is G, so length 1 impossible.
- From Level 1 neighbors, check direct connections to G:
  - Y–G: no.
  - B–G: no.
  - C–G: no.
Therefore length 2 is impossible. The found length-3 path is shortest.
